市場焦點
聯準會9月
升息一碼
• 聯邦基準利率升至 3.75%~4.00%，經濟預測部分上調2026-2028年GDP成長率、失業率皆下
調，通膨降溫進程仍相對緩慢 ，點陣圖顯示今年還有升息一碼的機會 ，這次升息對股票而言 ，
聯準會沒有準備以大幅犧牲經濟與就業換取通膨下降 ，降低了硬著陸風險；對債券而言 ，短
端仍須消化更高的政策利率，但長端反而可能受惠於抗通膨可信度改善。
AI模型
開發放緩
• Anthropic、OpenAI及xAI相繼支持放緩最先進AI模型開發速度。不過，前沿模型開發速度放
緩，並不代表AI基礎建設需求同步轉弱。晶片、算力、資料中心及能源等AI基礎建設仍處於供
不應求格局 ，短期若因市場情緒或資金槓桿調整而出現回檔 ，較可能反映估值與持倉修正 ，

而非AI需求基本面反轉 。後續若前期累積的槓桿壓力逐步消化 ，且全球總體經濟及地緣政治
風險未進一步惡化，AI供應鏈仍具中長期配置價值。
台灣央行
• 利率決議重點： (1)台灣央行宣布基準利率維持不變 ，重貼現率、擔保放款融通利率和短期融
通利率分別維持2%、2.375%和4.25%。(2)放鬆房市信用管制 ，調整自然人第2戶購屋貸款最
高成數上限，由6成調升為7成。(3)上修今年經濟成長率及通膨率 ，GDP上修至11.48%、CPI
及核心CPI分別上修至2.03%及2.16。(4)央行預估明年AI基礎建設需求將持續貢獻台灣出口表
現，不過由於基期已墊高，預測明年台灣經濟成長率為5.82%。

資料來源：Fed，華爾街見聞，2026.9.17
貨幣決議與9月、7月聲明稿差異，不再強調能源衝擊 全員支持升息
• 聯準會9月升息一碼，聯邦基準利率在3.75%~4.00%。
聲明稿重點差異
紅色字體為9月的新增部分，括號內藍色字體為刪除的7月聲明措辭
FOMC以12票贊成升息一碼 批准發布以下聲明 ：委員會決定 ，將聯邦基金利率目標 區間上調至 3.75%至4%維持在
3.50%至3.75%，以支持聯準會的雙重使命。委員會延續其維持銀行體系充足準備金的政策。
儘管中東衝突在一定程度上帶來了高水準的不確定性 ，美國經濟活動持續以穩健步調擴張 。儘管地緣政治情勢 等因素
使經濟前景仍籠 罩高度不確定性，但國內支出保持韌性，生產力成長強勁，企業資本投資也相當強勁穩健。新增就業

人數大致跟上勞動力成長速度，失業率也變化不大，顯示就業市場目前仍大致維持穩定。
相較於委員會2%的目標，通貨膨脹率依然偏高 ，這在一定程度上反映了部分特定部門 (包括能源)因供給衝擊所導致的
價格上漲。今日的政策行動，將有助於讓通膨更及時地回到2%目標，委員會將實現價格穩定。

聯準會會後記者會問答重點整理
1
通膨「過高且持續太久」 當
前重心偏向物價穩定
通膨數據未改善，通膨過高且持續太久，聯準會當前的政策重心偏向雙重使命中的物價穩定 。透過政策
行動表明，聯準會對抑制通膨是認真的。
2
經濟、就業與地緣政治促成
一致升息
近幾週公布的數據證實美國經濟正在走強 ，基礎經濟成長率高於先前判斷；其次 ，勞動市場依然穩健；
第三，通膨持續高於目標，地緣政治局勢也改變經濟展望 。華許表示，這三項因素共同促成當天「堅定
且一致」的決定。
3
點狀圖暗示今年再升 1碼 但
不是政策承諾
最新經濟預測顯示，多數官員認為今年仍有進一步升息空間。12 名官員預期年底前再升息 1 碼，另有 4
人預期還會升息 2 碼，僅 2 人主張利率維持在目前水準。不過，點狀圖反映的是官員對適當利率路徑的

個別預測，並非正式的承諾。
4
拒絕提供前瞻指引 不預判下
次會議結果
面對下一次升息可能落在 10 月或 12 月的追問，華許表示「我不給出前瞻指引。」
他強調，先前公開表達的是對貨幣政策紀律的承諾 ，而不是對特定利率決策的承諾 。聯準會不會預判未
來任何會議結果，後續行動仍將依據最新經濟數據及展望作出決定。
5
升息非市場逼迫 迴避川普施
壓問題
面對此次升息是否由債市波動及市場壓力所推動的提問，華許予以否認。
他表示是根據就業走勢、經濟強度及整體情勢作出決定 ，市場價格可作為觀察依據，但不能決定聯準會
的政策。
資料來源：Fed，華爾街見聞，2026.9.17

市場反應與投資建議
9/16
市場反應
• 市場將華許記者會解讀為偏鷹，那斯達克指數、標普
500指數、道瓊工業指數皆下跌。
• 10 年 期 美 債 殖 利 率 在 利 率 決 議 公 布 後 一 度 降 至
4.9323%，隨後回升至接近 4.99%，在關鍵的5%關
口附近波動。
• 2年期殖利率則由盤中低點4.5975%升至4.7124%，
反映投資人重新提高對後續升息的預期。
• 黃金跌破4,260美元。
• 利率市場顯示 ，交易員加大 聯準會在2026 年底前進
一步升息的押注，12月升息機率升至約50%。
投資建議
• 股市：AI長期趨勢不變，但需留意利率對評價面的影
響，逢拉回就分批買進，相對看好美、台、韓相關供
應鏈
• 債市：預期美債利率 維持高位震盪格局 ，目前債市

配置仍以鎖利為主，且聚焦中短天期。
資料來源：芝商所，2026.9.17

台灣利率連10凍 明年經濟展望佳
• 利率決議重點： (1)台灣央行宣布基準利率維持不變 ，重貼現率、擔保放款融通利率和短期融通利率分別維持 2%、2.375%
和4.25%。(2)放鬆房市信用管制，調整自然人第2戶購屋貸款最高成數上限，由6成調升為7成。(3)上修今年經濟成長率及通
膨率，GDP上修至11.48%(六月預估9.45 %)、CPI及核心CPI分別上修至2.03%及2.16%(六月預估1.91%及1.90%)。
• 觀點：全球AI資本支出維持上調的趨勢，台股供應鏈訂單直接受惠，央行預估明年AI基礎建設需求將持續貢獻台灣出口表現，
不過由於基期已墊高，預測明年台灣經濟成長率為5.82%。
• 台股短線展望：上週隨著聯準會利率決議公布後，台股量能回升，顯示短線消息面或已利空出盡。權值股台積電領軍台灣加

權指數站上月線，技術指標偏正向。投資佈局方面，由於地緣政治、通膨及貿易關稅風險仍存在不確定性，建議AI概念持股
核心布局行業龍頭企業，留意中小型成長股較易受到外部因素而股價大幅波動。
資料來源：中央銀行，2026.9.17。f代表央行預測數。

美國零售強勁回升擴大內需支撐，惟價格壓力與升息提高市場波動
• 零售全面回升，消費動能展現廣度：美國8月零售銷售月增1.2%，創3月以來最大增幅，優於市場預期，並扭轉7月修正
後月減0.5%；非實體店面銷售月增 2.6%、電子及家電增 1.6%，餐飲服務亦增 1.2%，且扣除汽車與汽油後仍增 1.2%，
控制組更增1.4%，顯示消費回升並非僅由汽車與燃料支出帶動，第三季內需仍具支撐。
• 名目消費偏強，通膨與升息增添需求壓力： 不過8月零售銷售年增6.0%屬名目增幅，部分反映物價上升因素；同期 CPI
年增3.4%，汽油價格月增3.9%，能源成本上升可能壓縮家庭實質購買力 ，加上Fed於9月決議升息1碼，後續利率政策
仍將視通膨與經濟數據調整，使市場焦點由消費回升轉向評估高利率與高物價對後續需求動能的影響。

• 後市觀察：短期零售回升仍有助支撐美國景氣，但若能源成本持續走高，加上升息後借貸成本上升，消費動能能否維持
將成為重要觀察點；投資配置可偏向美國投資級短中天期債券 ，兼顧收益與利率風險，股票則可聚焦獲利穩定、具定價
能力的大型優質企業，降低需求放緩與資金成本升高的影響。
近三月美國零售銷售月率變化(%)

顧 • AI安全監管升溫，短期壓抑市場情緒：Anthropic、OpenAI及xAI相繼支持放緩最先進AI模型開發速度，並引入獨立第三方
評估等安全機制，引發市場對AI資本支出降溫的疑慮。不過，前沿模型開發速度放緩，並不代表AI基礎建設需求同步轉弱。

科技企業自由現金流可望由目前約2,450億增至2028年的6,330億美元，支撐亞洲科技供應鏈中長期發展。短期若因市場情

緒或資金槓桿調整而出現回檔，較可能反映估值與持倉修正，而非AI需求基本面反轉。後續若前期累積的槓桿壓力逐步消化，
且全球總體經濟及地緣政治風險未進一步惡化，AI供應鏈仍具中長期配置價值。

油價在美國中期選舉前，或將維持在高檔
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.14
伊朗戰爭期間卡達油氣收入暴跌
卡達政府的石油和天然氣收入 美伊衝突
• 美伊衝突短期內仍難見明確解方，彭博預估雙方後續可能採取的行動，主要可分為以下四種情境：
(1)伊朗承受經濟封鎖：伊朗承受美國經濟制裁與施壓，避免進一步重大軍事升級。在美方護航行動支持下，商船仍可維持通行
荷姆茲海峽，國際原油供應未出現明顯中斷，油價預估在每桶80美元區間波動。
(2)伊朗將攻擊範圍限於海灣地區油輪與美軍：伊朗主要鎖定海灣地區能源運輸船隻及美軍目標，美方則採取有限度軍事回應，

衝突維持在一定範圍內，油價預估在每桶90美元區間波動。
(3)伊朗擴大攻擊範圍：攻擊目標不再局限於海灣地區船隻及美軍基地，海灣能源基礎設施以及紅海等重要航運咽喉，均可能成
為目標。局勢升級期間，油價可能維持在每桶100美元以上，待衝突緩和後回落至70至80美元區間，此情境發生的可能性最高。
(4)美伊雙方全面擴大開戰：伊朗進一步擴大攻擊目標，導致美國軍事資產及區域能源基礎設施遭受重大破壞，甚至不排除以色
列重新加入戰局，油價可能升至每桶120美元。

全球債市拋售勢頭不墜，AI融資競逐資金推升美債殖利率
• 長端殖利率走高，財政與通膨壓力仍是主因 ：近期美國長
天期公債殖利率持續走高，其中10年期美債殖利率更突破
5%，創2007年以來高點；除能源價格上升推升通膨疑慮
外，美國財政赤字、政府債務及持續性的發債需求 ，也提
高市場對長天期公債供給與期限溢酬的要求 ，反映長端利
率壓力仍未完全紓解。
• AI企業擴大融資，增加公司債供給與資金競逐： AI資料中
心、晶片與電力基礎建設的資本支出快速增加 ，帶動大型
科技企業提高債券融資規模；截至 6月，華爾街市場策略
研究機構Yardeni Research在追蹤外國私人投資人過去一
年買進美國公司債約3,900億美元，高於美國公債約3,290
億美元，顯示資金配置確有轉向公司債的現象 ，不過市場

研究亦指出，AI企業發債對美債殖利率的直接推升效果相
對有限，應可視為推動長天期利率上行的眾多因素之一。
• 後市觀察：若財政融資與AI基礎建設支出持續維持高檔 ，
長天期公債仍面臨較大的供給與資金配置壓力；但 AI投資
仍具中長期成長動能 ，配置上可關注資料中心 、半導體及
電力基礎建設供應鏈 ，固定收益則以短中天期美債及高品
質投資級公司債為主，降低長天期利率波動的影響。

新興市場本幣債券擁高票息提供防禦力
• 高票息提供防禦力：對債券投資人而言，票息就像安全墊。當市場利率上升、債券價格下跌時，持續收到的票息可以部分甚
至完全抵銷價格損失。新興市場本幣政府債券今年的表現，比市場原先預期更具韌性。目前新興市場本幣債券的持有收益約
為4.54%，已經足以對利率風險形成相當程度的保護 ，即使今年存續期間對多數市場造成負面影響 ，票息收益仍讓整體報酬
維持正數。這也是為什麼新興市場本幣債券在美伊衝突爆發後一度重挫超過 6%，但之後仍能逐步反彈 。哥倫比亞、巴西、
匈牙利及墨西哥的債券表現尤其突出。
• 市場已反映相當程度的升息風險：問題在於未來幾個月投資環境可能比上半年更加困難，能源價格上漲推升通膨，也迫使新
興市場央行維持較為鷹派的貨幣政策 。市場目前甚至預期 ，19個主要新興市場中 ，有18個市場的債券殖利率可能在年底前

上升。殖利率上升意味著債券價格承壓，因此投資人不能只看表面的高票息，必須同時評估未來利率變化可能造成資本損失。
新興市場本幣債券各因子對報酬率貢獻
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.15
新興市場本幣債券票息的貢獻度

新興市場本幣債券目前最大風險在於利率與匯率
• 存續期間仍是今年最主要的拖累因素：一般而言，債券的存續期間越高，當殖利率上升時，債券價格下跌的幅度就可能越大。
今年19個符合基準指數條件的新興市場中 ，有14個市場的總報酬受到存續期間拖累 ，其中韓國、馬來西亞、印尼、泰國及
菲律賓受到的負面影響最為明顯。更值得注意的是，新興市場債券的利率表現與美國公債高度相關，因此，投資新興市場債
券，不能只觀察當地央行，也必須密切留意美國聯準會利率政策及美國公債殖利率走勢。
• 匯率可能成為下一個重要變數：新興市場本幣債券除了債券價格本身，還存在另一項重要風險：匯率。今年即期匯率變動反
而對新興市場本幣債券帶來正面貢獻 ，約增加57個基點。但如果能源價格持續上漲並形成滯脹環境 ，順周期貨幣可能面臨

更大的貶值壓力 。目前市場預期年底前新興市場貨幣整體仍偏弱 ，19種新興市場貨幣中 ，有11種預計兌美元貶值 。其中土
耳其、哥倫比亞及巴西貨幣的潛在跌幅相對較大。這代表即使債券本身提供不錯的票息，如果當地貨幣大幅貶值，換算成美
元或其他投資人本國貨幣後，仍可能侵蝕甚至吞噬債券收益。
• 小結與投資建議：目前新興市場本幣債券比較像是高票息換取較高波動的投資，而不是單純追求資本利得的交易。在殖利率
已處於相對高檔、但利率與匯率風險仍未完全解除的情況下，採取分散市場、控制部位、逢回布局的策略會比追高更為合適。

資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.15

• 多重資產配置可有效風險管理：美伊衝突持續及聯準會利率政策，使今年金融市場震盪加劇，加上第四季美國期中選舉到來，
增加市場更多不確定性，不過從歷史經驗來看，地緣政治事件對股市的影響多屬短期現象，市場在初期情緒性下跌後，通常會
逐步回歸基本面，相較於預測事件發展，投資人更應透過多元資產配置分散風險，以提升投資組合的穩定性與長期抗震能力。
• 透過多重資產廣納收益來源：在利率維持相對高檔的環境下，投資人更應重視收益來源的多元化，從下圖可見，相較於過去幾
年，掩護性買權、非投資等級債，及投資等級債等資產的收益率均顯著提升，提供投資組合更具吸引力的現金流來源，其中掩
護性買權收益率已超過10%，非投資等級債，及投資等級債亦分別達7%及5%以上水準，透過結合不同收益型資產，不僅有助

提升整體收益潛力，更能降低對單一市場或利率變動的依賴，在市場波動加劇時提高投資組合的穩定度與韌性，兼顧收益與長
期成長目標。

6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重 。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。
11. 屬基金資訊觀測站ESG基金專區所列之基金，有關基金之ESG資訊，投資人應於申購前詳閱基金公開說明書或投資人須知所載之基金所有特色或目標等資訊。

国际油价上破 150 美元/桶概率较低，房价同比“转正”
时点是房地产投资增速企稳“观察锚”之一
——8 月经济数据解读
 8 月工业增加值当月同比 5.20%，预期 4.70%，前值 4.50%；8 月工业增加值
累计同比 5.30%，前值 5.30%；8 月固定资产投资完成额累计同比-7.20%，预

期-7.20%，前值-6.70%；8月社会消费品零售总额当月同比0.40%，预期0.70%，
前值 0.60%；8 月社会消费品零售总额累计同比 1.10%，前值 1.20%。我们解
读如下：
 一、国际油价上破 150美元/桶概率较低，明年 2 季度之后美联储加息概率不
大；
 二、8 月工业增加值数据总体表现尚可，但年内工业增加值当月同比或承压；
 三、房地产投资增速短期还将继续下探，房价同比“转正”时点是企稳“观
察锚”之一；
 四、城镇调查失业率上行，叠加美联储加息及 M2同比走弱，我国消费增速或
还将承压。
 风险提示：1.美伊战争持续时长超预期；2.美国通胀上行幅度高于预期。
 注：本文所有数据，均来自 WIND资讯，经英大证券整理。

### Page 3

宏观研究报告
请务必阅读最后一页的免责条款 3
事件：8 月工业增加值当月同比 5.20%，预期 4.70%，前值 4.50%；8 月工业增加值
累计同比 5.30%，前值 5.30%；8 月固定资产投资完成额累计同比-7.20%，预期-7.20%，
前值-6.70%；8月社会消费品零售总额当月同比 0.40%，预期 0.70%，前值 0.60%；8 月社
会消费品零售总额累计同比 1.10%，前值 1.20%。
解读：
一、国际油价上破 150美元/桶概率较低，明年 2季度之后美联储加息概率不大
国际油价剧烈波动是 2026年全球宏观经济面临的不确定性之一。当前国际油价位于
90-100美元/桶之间，主要支撑因素是美伊战争对原油供给的冲击，以及美国宏观经济在
2026年走强。在这两个因素的作用下，短期内国际油价还将位于一定高位。
当下资本市场关注的焦点之一是，本轮周期国际油价能否突破 150美元/桶。我们认
为国际油价突破 150美元/桶的概率较低。我们做出这一判断的依据来自需求与供给两个

方面。从需求端看：一是由于原油消费的刚性，短期之内国际油价上涨推升消费增速，
但是随着时间的推移，高油价对消费群体的收入形成较大压力，进而在中期反噬全球对
原油的需求，对国际油价形成利空；二是在 9 月美联储启动加息之后，后续还将继续加
息，而在加息之前美国联邦基金目标利率就已经位于相对高位，“高位加息”进一步推
升美国联邦基金目标利率，压制全球宏观经济，进而对国际油价形成利空。从供给端看：
一是波斯湾地区之外的原油生产，并未因为美伊战争封锁波斯湾而停止，相反，受全球
供给短期短缺以及连带的高油价的刺激，产油国大概率加大生产力度，以填补伊朗原油
缺位留下的全球供给缺口，这一点可以得到数据证实：4-8月，委内瑞拉原油月均产量在
100万桶的基础上不断上行，创 2019年 2 月以来新高，与此同时，美国原油月均产量持
续位于 1350万桶之上，且总体呈上行走势；二是随着时间的推移，海湾地区原油运输基
础设施不断建设与完善，体现在沙特阿拉伯的原油产量上：3月受美伊战争的影响，沙特
阿拉伯的原油产量从 2 月的 1011.2 万桶，快速下跌至 762.6万桶，在 4 月跌至 675.5 万

桶之后，总体呈上行走势，8 月录得 727.6 万桶。也就是说，随着时间的推移，伊朗对波
斯湾的封锁效应，以及连带的对国际油价的推升力度，大概率是减弱的。
图 1：委内瑞拉原油月均产量在 100 万桶的基础上不断上行（万桶）
数据来源：Wind资讯，英大证券整理
综上，我们认为在需求端中期边际收缩以及供给端中短期边际扩张的背景下，国际
油价在 2026 年 9 月至 2027年 12 月不具备冲击 150 美元/桶的潜力。鉴于国际油价走势

宏观研究报告

是美国 CPI同比的主导因素，因此在国际油价难以大幅冲高的假设下，我们认为 2027年
3月美国 CPI同比大概率承压下行，这将降低美联储加息的概率，增加美联储维持利率不
变的概率，并加大市场的降息预期。值得指出的是，根据历史经验，除非特殊情况，美
联储不会在加息结束后快速启动降息进程，叠加利率高位对美国宏观经济的压制效应需
要一段时间才能体现，尤其是美国 CPI同比可能会有一段时间的反复，因此美联储下一
次降息的时间点，可能位于 2027年 4季度或 4季度之后。
二、8 月工业增加值数据总体表现尚可，但年内工业增加值当月同比或承压
8月工业增加值数据总体表现尚可：一是 8月工业增加值当月同比录得 5.20%，高于
预期 0.50 个百分点，较前值大幅上行 0.70 个百分点；二是 8 月工业增加值累计同比录
得 5.30%，持平于前值，为 2024年 1 月以来低值；三是 8月工业增加值环比录得 0.54%，
较前值上行 0.43个百分点，创近 3 年同期新高。

分部类看，8 月采矿业、制造业与电力燃气及水的生产和供应业工业增加值当月同比
分别为-1.40%、6.10%、4.90%，分别较前值上行 2.80、0.60、-0.10 个百分点。其中：8
月制造业工业增加值当月同比较前值大幅上行0.60个百分点，创2025年10月以来新高，
是 8 月工业增加值当月同比的主要支撑项；8 月采矿业工业增加值当月同比虽然大幅上行
2.80 个百分点，是绝对读数依旧位于负值区间，且处于历史低位区间；8 月电力燃气及
水的生产和供应业工业增加值当月同比虽然较前值小幅下行，但绝对读数位于相对高位。
新动能方面，8月高技术产业工业增加值当月同比录得 16.70%，虽然较前值下行 0.20
个百分点，但是连续 7个月录得两位数增长，且为 2021年 9月以来次高值。与高技术产
业工业增加值当月同比相关联的一个指标是高技术制造业 PMI。从图形走势看，高技术制
造业 PMI 领先高技术产业工业增加值当月同比：高技术产业工业增加值当月同比的三个
低点分别是 2019 年 8 月的 6.10%、2020 年 10 月的 6.30%，以及 2023 年 7 月的 0.70%，

与之相对应，高技术制造业 PMI的三个低点分别是 2018年 12月的 48.10%、2020年 6 月
的 51.10，以及 2022年 12 月的 47.40，也就是说，高技术制造业 PMI 的三个低点分别领
先高技术产业工业增加值当月同比的三个低点 8 个月、4个月、7个月。
图 2：高技术制造业 PMI领先高技术产业工业增加值当月同比（%；%）
数据来源：Wind资讯，英大证券整理
在美联储加息的情况下，高技术制造业 PMI 是承压下行的，而在美联储降息的背景
下，高技术制造业 PMI 是上行的。当前美联储在高位加息，则高技术制造业 PMI大概率

### Page 5

宏观研究报告
请务必阅读最后一页的免责条款 5
会面临压力，进而对高技术产业工业增加值当月同比形成一定压力。当然，对高技术产
业工业增加值也面临利多。9月 26 日中美两国元首就中美建设性战略稳定关系及重大国
际地区问题深入交换意见，达成八点成果共识。其中，第七项与高技术产业直接相关，
即“双方同意建立中美人工智能对话，交流人工智能相关风险和惠益。下次对话将于今
年 11 月举行。双方同意建立人工智能事件的沟通渠道”，此外，第五项与高技术产业间
接相关，即“两国元首认可中美经贸磋商机制积极作用和双方经贸团队磋商成果，包括
建立并推进贸易理事会等机制，达成‘300亿美元’对等降税安排，延期吉隆坡经贸磋商
成果等，并指示予以落实。”值得指出的是，虽然高技术产业因为中美两国元首会晤面临
产业角度的利多，但是美联储加息使得高技术产业面临来自宏观角度的利空。我们认为，
宏观角度的利空可能大于产业角度的利多。因此，我们认为高技术制造业增加值当月同
比的上行速度，可能会有一定程度的放缓。在此背景下，叠加国内需求依旧疲弱，使得

年内工业增加值当月同比或承压。
三、房地产投资增速短期还将继续下探，企稳时点或可观察房价同比“转正”时点
8月房地产开发投资完成额累计同比为-19.90%，较前值下行 0.70个百分点，创 1999
年 2 月有数据记录以来新低，持续 49个月位于负值区间。
仅从销售端看，8 月商品房销售面积累计同比为-12.10%，较前值下行 0.30 个百分点，
持续 4 个月下探，表明 5 月至 8月商品房销售又出现一定的走弱。与此同时，8 月商品房
销售额累计同比录得-13.00%，连续 6 个月单边累计上行 7.20 个百分点，创年内新高，
但是要注意到，2026 年 1-8 月的整体情况，弱于 2025年全年的-12.60%。最后，8 月商
品房待售面积累计同比录得-1.10%，较前值下行 0.30个百分点，连续 6 个月位于负值区
间，创 2021年 6月以来新低，表明商品房的库存情况持续好转。综上，虽然面临商品房
待售面积累计同比进入负值区间的利多，但是商品房销售面积累计同比在负值区间深度

下探，预示房地产开发投资完成额累计同比在短期内还将继续下探。
以上判断得到来自居民端的佐证。从当月值的角度看，8 月居民户新增中长期贷款为
-822.00亿元，环比少减 380.00 亿元，同比多减 1,022.00亿元；从累计值的角度看，1-8
月居民户新增中长期贷款为 188.00 亿元。不论是当月值，还是累计值，均创有数据记录
以来的“新低”：8月-822.00亿元的读数，是 2007年-2026年同期的最低值，且是唯一
一个进入负值区间的月份；1-8月 188.00 亿元的读数，是 2007年-2026 年同期的最低值。
以上表明当前我国居民对房贷的需求较为疲弱。这一点也得到资金端的印证：房地产开
发资金来源中的个人按揭贷款累计同比在 8 月录得-22.40%，虽然较前值上行 1.10 个百
分点，但是连续 35个月位于负值区间。
目前的问题是，在房地产投资增速大概率还将继续承压的背景下，房价增速能否起
来？客观而言，在绝大多数时间里，房地产开发投资完成额累计同比与 70 大中城市二手

住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比是正相关的，且 70 大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数
当月同比领先房地产开发投资完成额累计同比，但是自 2024 年 10 月起，二者走势发生
了背离：70大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比在负值区间持续上行，但是房
地产开发投资完成额累计同比在负值区间持续下探。对这一现象的一个解释是，虽然 70
大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比上行，但是依旧位于负值区间，所以不能
有效带动房地产开发投资完成额累计同比企稳上行。一个假设是，如果 70 大中城市二手
住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比上行至正值区间，则房地产开发投资完成额累计同比有
企稳上行的可能。

### Page 6

宏观研究报告
请务必阅读最后一页的免责条款 6
图 3：房地产投资增速短期还将继续下探，企稳时点或可观察房价同比“转正”时点（%；%）
数据来源：Wind资讯，英大证券整理
接下来的问题是，70大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比有无可能升至正
值区间？8 月该值的读数为-5.10%，创近 30 个月新高。从内部结构看，8 月一线城市、
二线城市、三线城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比分别录得-2.70%、-4.90%、
-5.60%，分别较前值上行 1.0、0.20、0.20 个百分点。其中，一线城市的读数最高，且
向“零值”趋近。按照这个趋势，一线城市的房价同比有可能“转正”。在一线城市房价
同比领先二线城市与三线城市的背景下，二线城市房价同比存在转正的可能。一旦二线
城市房价同比“转正”，则 70 大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比大概率同时
“转正”，届时我们再观察房地产开发投资完成额累计同比是否能企稳。
四、城镇调查失业率上行，叠加美联储加息及 M2同比走弱，我国消费增速或还将承

压
8月主要失业率指标均不低于 7 月，表明 8月就业情况较 7 月出现边际走弱。具体看：
8 月城镇调查失业率录得 5.30%，较前值上行 0.10 个百分点，创近 5 个月新高；8 月 31
个大城市城镇调查失业率录得 5.30%，较前值上行 0.10个百分点，持平于 2026 年 3 月与
2025年8月，为2024年9月以来高点；8月本地户籍人口就业人员调查失业率录得5.30%，
较前值上行 0.10个百分点，创近 4 个月新高，为近 5 个月高点；8 月外来户籍人口就业
人员调查失业率录得 5.20%，持平于前值，为近 5 个月高点；8 月外来农业户籍人口就业
人员调查失业率录得 5.00%，较前值上行 0.10个百分点，创近 4 个月新高，为近 5 个月
高点。
分年龄组看，不含在校生的 8月 16-24岁劳动力、25-29岁劳动力、30-59 岁劳动力
城镇调查失业率分别录得 18.90%、7.50%、3.90%，分别较前值上行 1.0、0.30、0 个百分
点，其中，16-24岁劳动力城镇调查失业率持平于 2025年 8 月，为 2023年 12 月有数据

记录以来高点，25-29 岁劳动力城镇调查失业率创近 5 个月新高，而 30-59 岁劳动力城镇
调查失业率则处于历史低位区间。可以看出，初级劳动力面临更大的就业压力。
8月企业就业人员平均工作时间录得 48.20小时/周，连续 4个月持平于该值，为近
7个月高点。在城镇调查失业率系统性上升的背景下，企业就业人员平均工作时间依旧持
平于前值，表明在劳动力内部就业情况是冷热不均的：一部分人的工作时间上升，而另
一部分人的工作时间下降。从分年龄组城镇调查失业率的情况看，工作时间随着年龄的

### Page 7

宏观研究报告
请务必阅读最后一页的免责条款 7
增长而增加。这一现象对我国消费增速形成压力：相比 30-59 岁劳动力，16-24 岁劳动力
的工资收入低，且年轻人消费需求较为旺盛，边际消费倾向相对更高，也就是说，16-24
岁劳动力失业率持续上升，对消费增速形成更大的压力。
与 8 月城镇调查失业率系统性上升相对应，8 月消费数据系统性走弱。具体看：一是
8月社会消费品零售总额当月同比 0.40%，低于预期 0.30个百分点，较前值下行 0.20个
百分点，创近 4 个月新低；二是 8 月社会消费品零售总额累计同比 1.10%，较前值下行
0.10个百分点，创 2023年 2 月以来新低；三是 8 月社会消费品零售总额环比录得-0.13%，
较前值下行 0.14个百分点，时隔 2 个月再次进入负值区间，创近 4 个月新低。
我们认为，年内消费增速大概率还将承压。这一判断的依据，除了城镇调查失业率
上升之外，还来自两个方面。第一，年内美联储大概率至少再加息一次，这将对我国出
口增速形成一定程度的压制，尽管出口增速未必下行，进而对我国消费增速形成一定压

力，而从历史经验看，美联储加息利空我国宏观经济，进而利空我国消费增速。第二，8
月我国 M2 同比录得 7.50%，较前值下行 0.20 个百分点，创 2025年 4 月以来新低。这将
在一定程度上对我国消费增速形成压制。

9月 19日央行货币政策委员会召开 2026年第三季度例会。通稿删除第二季度例会的
“跨周期调节”表述，增加“综合运用并适时调整货币政策工具”，“不断完善工具设
计和管理”，“加快推动新旧动能转换”等表述。总体而言，第三季度例会表达出货币
政策“宽松”的倾向，这将对工业增加值增速、房地产投资增速以及消费增速形成支撑。

摩根⼠丹利将这⼀放缓定性为 " 暂停⽽⾮退潮 " ，预计四季度发⾏将回升，但不会重现上半年
的爆发式增⻓。该机构认为， 9 ⽉降温的原因并⾮基本⾯恶化或资本短缺 —— 前期发⾏⼤幅

摘要：
9 ⽉全球 AI 相关债券发⾏仅约 230 亿美元，为今年次低⽉份。美国投资级市场 AI 发⾏直接 " 挂零 " 。
摩根⼠丹利将这⼀放缓定性为 " 暂停⽽⾮退潮 " ，预计四季度发⾏将回升，但不会重现上半年的爆
发式增⻓。
截⾄ 9 ⽉底，今年全球 AI 相关债券发⾏总额已达 4660 亿美元，是去年全年 2160 亿美元的两倍以
上。
⼤摩在最新报告中分析称， 9 ⽉降温的原因并⾮基本⾯恶化或资本短缺 —— 前期发⾏⼤幅前置、
数据中⼼建设遭遇监管政治阻⼒、利率急升迫使项⽬层⾯条款重新谈判，三重因素叠加。
9 ⽉为何降温
今年 AI 融资的节奏极不均匀。 6 ⽉以 1130 亿美元的单⽉发⾏量创下全年峰值，随后逐⽉下⾏。

⾼质量超⼤规模云⼚商（⾕歌、亚⻢逊、 Meta 、微软）今年在投资级市场的发⾏额达约 1320 亿
美元，同⽐增⻓约 25 倍；加上甲⻣⽂和 SpaceX ，六⼤超⼤规模⼚商全⼝径（含⾮美元币种）发
⾏已达约 2540 亿美元，⼤量集中在上半年。摩根⼠丹利指出， " 经历了繁忙的夏季后，考虑到已
完成的发⾏量以及发⾏⼈建⽴发⾏节奏的努⼒，我们预期近期供给会放缓。 "
9 ⽉美国投资级市场 AI 发⾏为零。⾮美元市场略有活动，亚⻢逊发⾏了⼀笔 42.5 亿英镑的四批次
交易。杠杆融资⽅⾯，软银集团发⾏了约 100 亿美元⾼收益债券， AI 数据中⼼运营商 Crusoe 发⾏
了约 5 亿美元贷款， 9 ⽉杠杆融资总额约 140 亿美元。
第⼆重因素是监管和政治层⾯的约束。新数据中⼼建设⾯临许可证、电⼒供应和政治阻⼒ ——
摩根⼠丹利此前将其总结为 " 三个 P" ：⼈员（ people ）、电⼒（ power ）和政治（ politics ），这些
约束正在从潜在⻛险变为现实制约。
第三重因素是利率。收益率⼤幅上⾏使得项⽬层⾯的交易可能需要重新谈判，低评级发⾏⼈和

项⽬融资对资⾦成本更为敏感。
四季度展望：超⼤规模⼚商将回归，但节奏不同于去年
摩根⼠丹利预计四季度发⾏将超过 9 ⽉⽔平，但不会像去年那样集中爆发 —— 去年⼤部分供给出
现在四季度。
⾼质量超⼤规模云⼚商预计将重返美国投资级市场，同时继续在⾮美元市场融资。今年⾮美元
超⼤规模发⾏已达约 720 亿美元，接近全球超⼤规模发⾏总量的三分之⼀，币种从去年单⼀的美
元和欧元扩展⾄加元、英镑、瑞郎、澳元和⽇元。摩根⼠丹利预计四季度还将有欧元交易⼊
场。

（ ROIC ）前景强劲，债务融资成本仍低于股权融资。这些⼚商整体杠杆率仅 1.3 倍（净杠杆 0.4
倍），现⾦ / 债务⽐率 132% ，信⽤评级中位数 AA- ，资产负债表空间充裕。

宏观考验⼤于供给冲击
摩根⼠丹利强调，对投资级信⽤市场⽽⾔， " 宏观环境 —— ⽽⾮供给过剩 —— 是年底前最⼤的考
验。 "
投资级债券年内总回报已下跌 3% ，季度回报下跌 4% ，回撤幅度已达到可能触发共同基⾦和 ETF
⼤规模赎回的⽔平。 10 年期美债收益率从 1% 升⾄ 5% 以上，过去五年多次利率重定价对信⽤利
差和资⾦流造成了反复冲击。
不过，摩根⼠丹利认为信⽤市场整体能够消化收益率上⾏，因为名义增⻓率维持在 6% 以上、企
业盈利增速更强，利率上⾏有坚实的基本⾯⽀撑。该⾏经济学家预计美联储仅再加息两次，⽐
市场定价的接近四次更偏鸽派。
信⽤分化⽅⾯，摩根⼠丹利重申偏好有担保资产（约占 AI 相关债务的 30% ）。数据中⼼有担保债
券的利差波动主要由建设⻛险驱动 —— 许可证延迟、电⼒供应、租约不确定性，已稳定运营的
ABS 和 CMBS 资产受此类冲击较⼩。芯⽚融资的资产属性允许更快的现⾦流⽣成和摊还，但⽬前
公开交易的产品仍然有限。
展望四季度之后，摩根⼠丹利认为超⼤规模⼚商利差压缩交易的⼤部分⾏情可能已经⾛完：发

⾏节奏正趋于可预测，资本开⽀正向芯⽚等短久期资产转移， AI 投资的强劲回报也在持续验证⽀
出逻辑的稳健性。

## Document 153: [329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 - 기업

00

HD 현대중공업 (329180/KS) 
발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 
기다렸던 4 행정 중속 엔진 캐파 증설 단행 
HD현대중공업의 4행정 엔진 캐파는 기존 3.0GW에서 증설이 완료되는 '28년부터 
램프업을 거쳐 '30년 7.2GW까지 확대될 계획이다. ①[육상 발전용]은 신공장(울산 
온산) 3GW 와 HD 현대엔진(전남 영암) 1GW 를 합쳐 4.0GW, ②[선박용]은 
2.3GW에서 3.2GW로 확대된다. HD현대엔진 캐파는 기존 0.7GW에서 1GW까
지 확대되며, 해당 라인은 신공장이 가동되기 전 ‘27~’28 년 구간에서의 선박&육상 엔
진 모두 대응 가능하다. 신공장 캐파는 총 3.0GW로, ‘28년 약 30%의 가동률로 시
작해 램프업을 거쳐 ‘30 년부터 풀캐파로 20MW급 모델을 생산할 예정이다. 4 행정 
중속엔진에 대한 수요 가시성은 높다. 동사가 공유했던 육상 발전 엔진 인콰이어리 규
모는 기존 약 3~9GW였으나, 현재는 이를 크게 상회하는 수준으로 소통하고 있다.

최근 부유식 데이터센터(FDC)에 대한 하이퍼스케일러들의 인콰이어리 또한 확대되고 
있다. HD 한국조선해양은 최근 미국 선급(ABS)로부터 100MW급 규모의 부유식 발
전 설비(BMPP)에 대한 기본 인증(AiP)를 획득했으며, 동사는 FDC 신조 사업과 더불
어 발전원 4 행정 엔진을 파워십 및 부유식 발전설비(BMPP)에 납품하며 그룹사들과 
패키지 사업으로 진행할 예정이다. 가시화된 수요를 기반으로 엔진기계사업부의 구조적 
성장 보여줄 것으로 전망하며, 이에 따른 리레이팅을 예상한다.

투자의견 ‘매수’ 및 목표주가 기존 97 만원 유지 
HD 현대중공업에 대한 투자의견 ‘매수’ 및 목표주가 기존 97 만원을 유지한다. 동사는 
올해 상선수주 목표 이미 초과달성하며 안정적인 펀더멘털 유지 중이다. 하반기 남은 
기간은 LNGC, VLGC/VLAC 중심의 선별 수주 전략을 통해 ‘28 년 이후의 상선 부
문 수익성 유지 충분히 가능할 것으로 예상된다. 글로벌 지정학적 리스크 확대에 따른 
해군 현대화 사이클 기조에 맞추어 미 해군은 동맹국의 호위함 구매를 고려하고 있으
며, MASGA를 기반으로 중장기적으로 미국 전투함을 건조하기 위해 HD현대의 미국 
진출 가능성 높다는 판단이다. 또한 미국을 제외한 글로벌 함정(페루, 필리핀, 말레이시
아, 칠레, 사우디, 이집트 등) 수주 파이프라인도 다수 보유하고 있어 함정 모멘텀에 따
른 리레이팅 충분히 가능할 전망이다. 특수선+4 행정 엔진+FDC+SMR 등 조선소에 
붙일 수 있는 모든 추가 성장동력을 보유한 유일한 종목이기에 조선업 최선호주(Top-
Pick)로 제시한다. 최근 불거지고 있는 노조 파업 문제 해소될 시 저점 매수 기회라는 
판단이다.

## Document 169: Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• The ongoing oil-price shock shouldn’t prove structural, with flows likely to normalize in 
2027. But several pressure points are pulling oil and gas prices in different directions 
and the balance will decide where prices go next.  Brent jumped to nearly 110 USD/bbl 
after Saudi Arabia was forced to shut its East-West pipeline, pushing the net hit to Hormuz 
flows to about 4.3mbpd vs pre -war levels.  For now, pressure points (including strategic 
reserves near historic lows  and an underinvested refining base) are outweighing the two 
relief valves (shadow traffic in Hormuz and demand destruction). As we expect the Saudi 
pipeline to be repaired rather swiftly and volumes steady in Hormuz,  Brent should average 
87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before consolidating to 76 USD/bbl in 2027. But prices would hold 
near 100 USD/bbl if the outage extends toward six weeks and Hormuz remains in flux.  
• European gas prices are climbing as the region races to refill, though the resulting 
macro risk looks contained for now. Gas prices have rallied from the mid-40 EUR/MWh in 
July to over 80 EUR/MWh as Hormuz LNG loadings stay stuck at 15-25% of pre-war levels. 
We expect TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh in Q4 2026 before easing to 36 EUR/MWh in 2027 
amid larger supply from both the US and the Middle  East. A sustained move above 120  
EUR/MWh for six months would risk tipping the Eurozone into recession.  
• A separate refining and export bottleneck is keeping refined product prices elevated. 
US and European crack spreads have surged to 62 EUR/bbl and 74 EUR/bbl. On top of 
Hormuz disruptions, a significant share of Russia’s refining capacity has been  hit by 
Ukrainian strikes, resulting in  a diesel export ban. Until June, China also had export 
restrictions in place amid fear s of supply shortage s. A structurally underinvested refining 
base is adding to the multi -layered crisis . As a result,  global oil demand is expected to 
contract 2.5mbpd in 2026 as demand from Asia has collapsed. 
• Diesel-reliant transport and agriculture, Asian petrochemicals and fertilizers are the 
most exposed sectors  with very few short -term substitutes available , while producers 
with domestic feedstock flexibility are comparatively insulated.  Between March and 
August, fossil -fuel importers paid an estimated extra USD330bn. High d iesel prices are 
hitting trucking, agriculture and construction, and the Gulf’s 40% share of global sulfur 
exports is impacting food production via fertilizer s. The n ear-term adjustment is mainly 
through inventory drawdowns and output cuts rather than genuine substitution.  
• Households are only starting to feel the full inflationary hit, and the pass -through will 
remain sticky into 2027.  In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of August’s CPI 
increase, with pump prices set to reach 3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y, about 355 USD 
more per household annually). In Europe, regulated tariffs are catching up with a lag and 
lower-income households remain disproportionately exposed.  
• Bonds are already pricing  in most of the  energy premium, but sustained inflation and 
oil-price spikes could lead to further rate stress. Much of the initial repricing has already 
occurred. We estimate that a further increase in Brent to 120 USD/bbl would only lift yields 
by 5-10bps (vs 17-18bps on inflation swaps and 8-11bps on expected policy-rate pricing). 
This suggests that a significant further rise in yields would likely require a renewed large 
supply shock or evidence that inflation is delaying monetary -policy normalization. 
• Looking ahead, over the longer run, the unfolding events  are fundamentally a 
Hormuz/Gulf shock  that will reshape global energy  markets. The conflict is reshaping 
global energy trade routes: Gulf states are building out bypass infrastructure (ADCOP, the 
Kirkuk-Ceyhan/Development Road corridor, Duqm) while China, India, Japan and South 
Korea diversify away from Gulf -transiting barrels; LNG has no equivalent bypass, so gas 
exposure remains structural.

Chokepoints and pressure points : what is breaking 
and reshaping global energy markets

### Page 2

Roller-coaster oil markets  
The recent attack on Saudi Arabia’s East -West pipeline (Petroline), combined with stalled talks between Iran 
and Gulf states on Hormuz shipping, is compounding risks, but we expect the shock to fade. Brent has spiked to 
nearly 110  USD/bbl, widening an already -significant Persian Gulf supply gap (see Figure 1): despite lower Gulf 
production, exports proved more resilient, but the net hit to Hormuz flows still stands at about 4.3mbpd versus pre -
war levels. It remains uncertain when the S audi pipeline will resume normal flows of 7mbpd - a mild scenario sees 
volumes ramp back to 5-6mbpd within weeks, while a worst case keeps the pipeline shut for about six week but we 
see this as a temporary shock either way. 
Figure 1: Supply gap estimate (mbpd)

*

 , Allianz Research

OECD commercial stocks remain the main shock absorber, but that buffer is thinning fast. US Strategic Petroleum 
Reserve draws have slowed to just 0.3mbpd over the past month - reserves are now near their lowest level in 
decades, so Washington has little scope to repeat the scale of releases seen in 2022  or earlier this year even if the 
deficit widens further ; while Japan and South Korea are rebuilding their own reserves rather than drawing them 
down. China’s crude imports, meanwhile, are only just restarting after months of weakness and remain about 
4mbpd below year-ago levels; refiners are taking advantage of firm margins and spare quota to buy and reprocess 
crude for re-export rather than to meet stronger domestic demand, so this is not yet a genuine demand recovery. 
Against this backdrop, we forecast Brent averaging 88 USD/bbl in Q3 and 87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before easing to 
76 USD/bbl in 2027 - but a deeper or more prolonged Saudi export disruption, without an offsetting reserve release, 
would keep the market in deficit for longer and raise the probability of Brent holding near 100  USD/bbl.

A natural gas squeeze looming over Europe? 
Natural gas prices are ticking up as Europe races to refill storage.  European gas is exposed to the same Middle 
East disruption through a different channel: LNG loadings through Hormuz have remained stuck at 15 -25% of pre-
war levels for two months, even as Qatar sends more empty tankers into the Gulf. We expect Qatari and Emirati 
LNG exports to normalize only in Q1 2027, so for TTF to keep pricing enough Asian LNG demand out of the market 
to free cargoes for Europe, prices must stay elevated for longer: the rally from the mid -40 EUR/MWh in July to over 
80 EUR/MWh has already cut some demand from the rest of the world. With Europe still playing catch -up on 
storage (see Figure 2), we expect TTF to average 65 EUR/MWh in both Q3 and Q4 2026 before consolidating to 36 
EUR/MWh in 2027 as Hormuz flows normalize and incremental US LNG supply arrives. The key near -term risk is 
storage: a cold winter combined with a lower-than-usual fill could deplete storage before spring, front-loading price 
pressure into early 2027. 
Figure 2: European gas storage

### Page 4

4

For now, the macro risk from gas prices looks contained.  In 2022, gas prices averaged close to 130 EUR/MWh for 
a full year, yet the Eurozone still grew about 3.4%, cushioned by a post -pandemic demand boom and a fiscal 
response worth an estimated 0.4pp of growth. Today’s backdrop is less forgiving: fiscal space has narrowed and 
household demand is far less dynamic. We think TTF in the 80 -100 EUR/MWh range could be absorbed for nine 
months or more without a growth shock, but a sustained move above 120 EUR/MWh for six months or longer would 
tip the Eurozone into recession. Our base case, TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh through Q3 and Q4 2026, does not test 
that threshold, though a winter upside scenario would keep the tail risk alive.  
Refined products to remain expensive  
A two -layer infrastructure bottleneck is hitting refined products and will keep driving regional prices up, 
independent of crude.  In August, Brent averaged 89  USD/bbl, yet clean -product crack spreads surged to record 
levels, the US benchmark reached 62  EUR/bbl and European diesel crack 74 EUR/bbl (68% and 92% above their 
five-year averages) showing the main story is refining availability, not crude input costs. Three regions are driving 
the crunch. In the Middle East, the US -Iran conflict and Hormuz blockade have hit both refinery operations and 
product exports: pre -conflict flows of roughly  6mbpd through the Strait have fallen to 1.1mbpd, and the Saudi 
pipeline attack compounds this by also halting flows to the Yanbu refineries. In Russia, Ukrainian strikes cut refinery 
runs to multi -year lows by mid -May, and Moscow’s diesel export ban - extended through September, with no 
guarantee against further extension - has squeezed a market that depends on Russia for 11% of global diesel. In 
China, export restrictions imposed in March to preserve domestic inventories cut clean -product exports by 24.3% 
year-on-year over the first five months, though Beijing’s June release of 13mn tons should ease global supply, lifting 
China’s exports full-year total to 32mn tons, 0.6% above last year. 
Figure 3: US and Europe crack spread historical prices (USD/bbl)

Sources: 
 LSEG Workspace
 , Allianz Research

Global clean-product inventories are drying up as the crisis extends through 2026.  European (ARA) inventories 
are well below the five -year average. In the US, refiners are running at record utilization (98% in September), but 
most of the extra output is being exported for higher margins rather than sold domestically — gasoline stocks are 
6% and diesel 14% below seasonal norms, and US diesel topped 6  USD/gallon for the first time. Rising domestic 
prices could eventually pull barrels back into the US market and curb these exports, with some early – tough strongly 
pushed back by government officials for now  - talks about potential diesel export restrictions to support domestic 
supply. Additionally, there is little relief from spare capacity: years of underinvestment and refinery closures have 
left the West structurally unprepared, global refining investment is on track to fall below 40  USD bn this year (-15% 
y/y), and capacity is set to grow only marginally through 2040. Unlike 2022, when Europe and Asia reallocated 
capital toward LNG regasification within months, refining capacity cannot be added quickly enough to ease this 
shortage in the short or medium term, and rebuilding inventories will take time even once Hormuz flows normalize. 
We now expect world oil demand to contract by 2.5mbpd in 2026 as high product prices force further demand 
destruction, concentrated in middle distillates and petrochemical feedstocks in Asia. Longer term, persistently 
expensive products should accelerate substitution toward EVs and heat pumps, and put a premium on refineries 
with the flexibility to shift crude grades and product mix.

Downstream sector impact and potential substitution 
After six months, duration, and not just the peak price, is now the central macro risk. Between March and August, 
fossil-fuel importers paid an estimated 330  USD bn more than pre-war forward prices implied, split roughly evenly 
between crude and refined products - a real income transfer from importers to exporters that squeezes household 
purchasing power and corporate margins, weakens currencies, and strains public finances wherever governments 
subsidize fuel. The Hormuz crisis is unusual in disrupting many linked -but-hard-to-substitute commodities at once 
(crude, gas, LPG, naphtha and sulfur ) spilling into fertilizer and petrochemical shortages. Substitution can happen 
at three levels : (i) switching energy carriers, (ii) switching industrial feedstocks, or (iii) consumers switching final 
products. With the first working fastest but simply redistributing scarcity, and the other two developing more slowly 
but reshaping industrial structure permanently. Inflation itself spreads in three stages: a fast first -round hit to 
energy-intensive industries (speed depending on taxes and subsidies), a slower production -cost effect as old-price 
inventories and contracts run out, and a second -round effect as wages, inflation expectations and price -setting 
behavior adjust. 
The immediate impact is on energy -intensive industries.  Gulf states are major exporters of both fuels and 
feedstocks: Europe is less directly exposed to Gulf crude than East Asia but still feels it through global oil prices, LNG 
competition and product shortages; the US is more insulated on crude but exposed via global product prices, hitting 
lower-income consumers hardest while producers and refiners gain from higher margins. East and South Asia face 
the largest physical exposure, with China, India and Japan recording the highest net additional fossil -fuel import 
costs since the conflict began – USD 31.7 bn, USD 14.4bn and USD 10.4bn respectively. Diesel is the most powerful 
transmission channel given its role in trucking, agriculture, mining and construction - sectors that are hard to 
electrify, especially for long -haul transport and in emerging markets - while shipping and aviation face higher 
bunker/jet-fuel costs and rerouting. Among industrial inputs, East Asian petrochemical crackers reliant on imported 
naphtha are most exposed, whereas the US benefits from domestic ethane and China retains partial cover via coal-
based chemicals; metals, cement and glass are exposed through electricity and high-temperature heat costs, hitting 
aluminum hardest. Gulf countries supply more than 40% of global sulfur exports and about 20% of 
ammonia/nitrogen-fertilizer exports, so the crisis strikes food production through fertilizer, diesel, irrigation and 
packaging costs simultaneously. 
The second-round effect on the broader economy will be felt with a lag and last longer.  Food shows this best: 
packaged food embeds fertilizer, farm fuel, irrigation, refrigeration, processing heat, packaging, transport and 
labor, each repricing at a different speed : fresh produce reacts within weeks, grain can take up to a year via fertilizer 
and harvest effects, and restaurant prices adjust slowest but most persistently as labor and rent costs catch up. 
Large retailers can hedge and absorb costs better than small one s, and real household income falls as consumers 
trade down and defer spending. Export restrictions, subsidies and price controls can temporarily shield domestic 
food prices but tend to redistribute scarcity and worsen the burden on lower -income, food-importing economies.

### Page 6

6

Construction and real estate are hit both directly (cement, plastics, metals, diesel) and, more durably, indirectly 
through higher interest rates raising financing costs and lowering the present value of future rents. Compared with 
2022, the economy enters this shock with more renewable capacity and European LNG infrastructure - renewables 
installed since 2020 reportedly avoided USD 36bn of fossil -fuel imports in the first five months of the crisis - but 
public debt is higher and buffers are thinner after the pandemic and the 2022 crisis.  
Substitution will spread across fuels, feedstocks and products but initially redistributes inflation rather than 
eliminating it. The first response is inventory drawdowns, switching to costlier available inputs, output cuts and cost 
pass-through. Over time it becomes more structural, reorganizing downstream markets around flexibility, lower 
energy intensity and redundancy - persistently expensive oil and gas improve the economics of electrification, even 
though electrification changes commodity dependence rather than removing it. Because capital, regulatory 
approval and margins all constrain the pace of change, expect a two -stage adjustment: curtailment and demand 
destruction first, structural substitution and capex later , alongside rising demand for fuel/feedstock flexibility even 
where it costs more than a specialized, single-fuel plant. 
Figure 5a and 5b: Fuel potential substitution in energy-intensive sectors in the short-term; energy-intensive sector 
inputs (left) within share of total inputs in specific sectors (% - right)

Source: Allianz Research
 , Eurostat, Oxford Economics  
Households to face higher inflation 
Six months in, households are losing purchasing power in stages.  Petrol and diesel reprice within days or weeks, 
but gas and electricity bills follow regulated tariff calendars and contract -renewal dates, so the current inflation 
reading is not yet the peak. Exposure varies sharply by household - an EV- and heat-pump-owning homeowner is 
comparatively insulated, a gas-heated renter won’t feel the full effect until tariffs reset, and a rural household reliant 
on diesel cars feels it immediately. Petrol and diesel are the fastest channel: European pu mp prices typically pass 
through crude costs within one to two months, roughly one -for-one. In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of 
August’s CPI increase, diesel peaked above 6  USD/gallon in September, and average pump prices are on track for 
3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y) - about USD 355 more per year for a typical household; hitting rural, low-income 
households hardest given limited access to public transport.  
Gas and electricity bills rise on institutional timetables, so the pass -through is felt with a lag that varies by 
market. In France, the regulated gas reference price rises 6.3% on October 1 (to 182.88  EUR/MWh including tax, 
about 60 EUR/year for indexed contracts), though roughly 40% of households are on fixed-price contracts and won’t 
feel it until renewal; French electricity moves more slowly, with the next adjustment scheduled for February 2027. 
The UK’s quarterly price cap rises by a bout 5  GBP/month from October, partly offset by government VAT relief, 
while roughly 35% of households on fixed contracts are shielded for now. Japan and India rely more on subsidy 
calendars than market pass -through, which smooths the household shock across time wit hout removing its fiscal 
cost. More broadly, real energy bills were still about 4% above 2019 levels in 2024, so this shock lands on budgets 
that had not fully recovered from 2022 ; and unevenly: an estimated 120mn households in advanced economies 
spend over 10% of income on home energy, rising to 20% including transport for the poorest, and the bottom income 
quintile spent 12% of disposable income on gas, electricity and heating in 2022 versus 4% for the top quintile.

In the longer term, the energy crisis impacts inflation beyond energy bills.  A second inflationary wave builds as 
transport and industrial costs feed into food, goods and services with a lag of around two years: a permanent 10% 
rise in oil prices raises headline CPI by about 2.3%, food CPI by 0.3% and core CPI by 0.1%, and after e xpenditure 
weights, headline CPI rises by almost 0.4% - consistent with the roughly 0.5% inflation contribution the last oil move 
added in the Eurozone, UK and Canada. Looking ahead, households are likely to weigh energy resilience more 
heavily in purchasing decisions, shifting spending from variable fuel cost s toward upfront capex on electrification 
and insulation - even as higher prices simultaneously squeeze the ability to finance that switch, widening inequality 
in energy exposure. Electricity is set to become the central household energy price, trading oil and gas exposure for 
exposure to power prices, network ch arges and time -of-use pricing, with government subsidies and price -cap 
calendars still deciding when the crisis is actually felt.

The energy-yield loop 
Energy shocks often have an immediate effect on rates through several components.  The first one is inflation 
expectations: Higher commodity prices tend to increase inflation and are priced directly on the derivatives market . 
Historically a 1% rise in Brent lifts the 2Y inflation swap by 1. 65bps in the US and 1. 77bps in the Eurozone, and the 
1y1y forward inflation swap by 0.64bp in the US and 1.09bp in the Eurozone. During special shocks the effect 
intensifies: The US 2Y inflation swap moved 1. 86bps per 1% of oil during the Ukraine shock and 2. 01bps during the 
Iran episode. The EU 2Y inflation swap moved by 2.60bps during the Ukraine shock, but only 0.36bp during the Iran 
episode. 
The first channel then impacts  the expected short rates path.  When inflation tends to increase suddenly, the 
central bank faces more pressure  to be hawkish because of market pricing . Historically a 1% rise in Brent adds 
1.02bp to US monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y OIS − Fed Funds) and 0.83bp to EUR monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y 
OIS − €STR). During crises , this pressure concentrates region by region: US pricing repriced hardest during the 
Ukraine shock at 2.82bps per 1% of oil, close to three times its long -run sensitivity, while in the current Iran episode

### Page 8

it is EUR pricing that dominates at 2.40bps. This second channel tends to move short rates (more sensitive to inflation 
and monetary policy) (<2Y maturities: US 2Y +0.95bp, DE 2Y +0.53bp per 1% of oil) and the "belly" (more sensitive 
to growth and the economy) (5Y –7Y: US 5Y +0.98bp, DE 5Y +0.54bp) fa ster than long rates (which embed more 
fundamental factors such as demography, fiscal situation, political stability) (>10Y maturity: US 30Y +0.65bp, DE 
30Y +0.50bp). When the front end increases more than the long end, it induces a bear flattening , which hurts the 
economy more broadly. This tends to tighten financial conditions by increasing borrowing costs, slowing the growth 
of production and credit, but also by increasing transport costs, making it harder for commodities trading houses to 
provide more inputs to markets due to their short -term financing at 3M.

In times of shocks, the sensitivities of commodities on rates roughly double . On the German curve, a 1% rise in 
Brent moves the 2Y yield by 0.53bp historically versus 1.43bp in the current episode, and EUR monetary pricing by 
0.83bp versus 2.40bp. Per +10% move in Brent, that is about +5bps on the German curve and +9bps on the US curve 
in normal times, rising to about +14bps on the German 2Y and +12bps on the US 10Y in shock, with the 2Y inflation 
swaps moving some + 17bps historically and up to + 20bps ina  shock.  Figure 9 illustrates the incremental impact 
on rates and inflation markets implied by a move in Brent to USD120, USD100 or USD80 from current levels. 
These scenario moves are separate from the repricing already observed during the current episode, much of 
which is already embedded in inflation compensation and monetary-policy expectations.

### Page 9

9

Bond markets have already absorbed a large portion of the current energy shock through higher inflation 
compensation and a repricing of the expected monetary -policy path. While additional oil-price gains would still 
exert upward pressure on rates, the scenario analysis suggests that the marginal impact is now smaller than during 
the initial phase of the shock. As a result, a sustained and significant further rise in yields would likely require a new 
escalation in supply disruptions or clearer evidence that energy -driven inflation is feeding into broader price 
dynamics and delaying the normalization of monetary policy.  
How the conflict is reshaping the global energy map 
The Strait of Hormuz has always been the Gulf’s structural vulnerability, and this crisis is accelerating a shift 
from managing chokepoint risk to actively engineering around it.  The attack on Saudi Arabia’s own hedge (i.e.  
Petroline) is a reminder that existing bypass infrastructure is now a target, not a guarantee. Riyadh’s repair timeline 
will determine how quickly that redundancy returns, but the episode has already revived talk of expanding capacity 
rather than just repairing it. The UAE’s ADCOP pipeline to Fujairah, which sits outside the Strait entirely, remains the 
cleanest working bypass and has absorbed a large share of redirected flows, with further capacity additions 
planned for 2027. Iraq is leaning on the Kirkuk -Ceyhan pipeline and the broader Development Road corridor to 
Türkiye, while Oman’s Duqm port is the other candidate for a Gulf pipeline that bypasses the Strait altogether.

There is no easy fix for natural gas.  Unlike crude, LNG cannot be pipelined around Hormuz, which is why Qatari 
and Emirati LNG loadings have collapsed even as oil exports partially recovered via redirection. With normalization 
pushed out to Q1 2027, Qatar’s pattern of sending more empty tanke rs into the Gulf is one of the few signs of an 
eventual ramp-up, but there is no Petroline-equivalent for gas and the exposure is structural. 
On the demand side, the pressure to de -risk predates this crisis — it dates back to the war in Ukraine — and is 
now intensifying, though Russia/Ukraine plays no direct role in the current Hormuz shock beyond the refined -
products channel already noted.  China has spent the past decade building overland alternatives (the ESPO 
pipeline from Russia and the Kazakhstan -China link) specifically to cut seaborne Gulf and Malacca dependency, 
and its crude imports remain about 4mbpd below 2025 levels even at firm prices — though the recent uptick in 
Chinese buying is a refining -margin and re -export story more than fresh demand. India is diversifying toward US, 
West African and discounted Russian barrels via the Cape route while expanding strategic reserves, and Japan and 
South Korea are rebuilding SPRs drawn down earlier in the crisis. The India -Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor 
is likely to see fresh political momentum as a longer-term, non-maritime link between the Gulf and Europe. None of

### Page 10

10

this displaces Hormuz as the region’s central artery near -term, but it marks a durable shift toward supply -side 
redundancy and demand-side diversification that will outlast the current conflict.

## Document 216: Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13023135     1

Economy 
Entering the age of the older consumer 
01 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• The older consumer is moving center stage. America's population has been aging for decades, but the shift has accelerated: the 
share aged over 60 increased by almost 10 percentage points (pp) between 1995 and 2025. And the Census Bureau projects the 
share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population. 
• More free time means a different spending mix. People aged 65 and over spend around two and a half fewer hours per day 
working and roughly two hours more on leisure than the overall adult population. Bank of America data shows that older 
households allocate relatively more of their card spending to groceries and travel, and less to restaurants, gasoline, general 
merchandise and clothing. 
• Households aged over 55 held close to $140 trillion in net worth in the second quarter of 2026, giving many older consumers 
considerable capacity to spend on travel and other leisure categories. But wealth is not evenly distributed, and healthcare and 
other cost pressures remain significant for older households on tighter or fixed incomes.

Population aging is nothing new, but it is accelerating  
The US population has been growing relatively “older” for a long time. Exhibit 1 shows that the proportion of the population 
under 20 years old shrank from the mid-1960s through 2025. At the same time, the proportion of the population in older age 
groups has been rising.  
Exhibit 1: Over time the US population has steadily aged  
Proportion of US population by age range (%)

Source: Human Mortality Database; Bank of America Institute calculations

Additionally, there has been a notable acceleration in the relative aging of the population in recent years. For example, between 
1995 and 2025, the proportion of the population aged over 60 rose by almost 10 percentage points (pp) to 25%.  
Several factors have driven this. First, fertility rates in the US, as in much of the world, have fallen sharply. This has slowed 
natural population growth and, by reducing the number of younger people relative to older cohorts, has increased the share of 
the population at older ages. Second, more recently, net inward migration to the US has slowed. Because migrants tend to be 
younger than the overall population, weaker immigration also tends to raise the average age. Finally, rising life expectancy over 
much of the post-war period has allowed more people to survive into older age, further increasing the older population's share.  
Exhibit 2: The trend towards an older composition of the population is expected to continue  
Forecast changes in the share of population by age group relative to 2025 (pp)

Source: United States Census Bureau 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Lower fertility and, more recently, weaker net migration look set to slow overall US population growth in the decades ahead. As a 
result, older Americans are likely to play an increasingly important role in the economy, both as a share of the population and, in 
many age groups, in absolute numbers. The Census Bureau projects the share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 
2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population (Exhibit 2). For businesses, understanding how the needs and spending patterns 
of older consumers differ from those of younger generations will therefore become increasingly relevant. 
Older age groups allocate their time differently  
Why does this matter? One reason is that older people tend to spend their time and money quite differently from younger adults. 
Most obviously, many work less or have retired altogether. Data from the American Time Use Survey (ATUS) shows that people 
aged 65 and over spend, on average, around two and a half fewer hours per day working than the population aged 15+ (Exhibit 
3). 
That leaves considerably more time for other activities. People aged 65 and over spend around two hours more per day on 
leisure and sports activities, with television viewing accounting for a particularly large share of this additional leisure time 
(Exhibit 4).

Older households also spend differently  
Bank of America internal data on credit and debit card spending provides insights into how these differences in older 
generations’ use of time also impact their spending. Rather than splitting our spending data by generation, as we often do, here 
we split by age bands, allowing for consistent comparisons over time.  
Exhibit 5 shows how the composition of card spending of households aged 61-75 and those older than 75 differs from that of all 
households. A few things stand out. For one, older households devote a greater share of their overall card spending to groceries 
(food and beverages). Households aged 61-75 also allocate more spending to travel (airlines and lodging). And restaurants and 
bars, gasoline, general merchandise and clothing account for a smaller share of older households’ card spending.  
These differences may seem fairly intuitive. Older households have more leisure hours, so spend relatively more on travel to 
enjoy them. Previously we found that more available time may support travel particularly around retirement, although the 
discretionary spending lift can be temporary (for more, read our publication: Paychecks to pensions: The evolution of retiree 
spending).  
Older households are also likely to have less need to update their work wardrobes, so they probably spend less on clothing.

Still, older households’ relatively lower spending on restaurants and bars is a little more curious, perhaps, given dining out could 
be one way of making use of more leisure time. Indeed, when we look at how these spending shares are evolving, we find a 
nuanced picture. While households aged 61-75 do spend relatively less on restaurants and bars than the overall population, they 
appear to be narrowing this gap. But for those older than 75 this is not the case. In our view, the pandemic may still be weighing 
on dining-out spending among this older age group (Exhibit 6).

Aging presents opportunities, but also challenges 
In our view, the aging of the US population presents important opportunities for the economy and businesses. Older consumers 
have more leisure time and businesses that cater to these demands may therefore benefit. 
Moreover, some older consumers hold considerable amounts of net wealth and have been some of the biggest beneficiaries of 
recent rises in equities. Using data from the Federal Reserve, Exhibit 8 shows that as of the second quarter of 2026 the net 
worth of those households aged 55 and over was close to $140 trillion, around three-quarters of the total. And with equity 
markets rising strongly, these age groups have seen a more than 20% increase in their net worth over the past two years, 
potentially enhancing their spending power further.  
But an important caveat is that not all older consumers are in this position. Previous Institute work found evidence consistent 
with equity-market wealth effects being concentrated toward higher-income households (read our publication: Paychecks to 
pensions: The evolution of retiree spending). And Census Bureau research has found that around 14% of Social Security 
recipients aged over 65 rely on it for over 90% of their income. For older households without a wealth cushion, recent elevated 
cost pressures, like gasoline, will likely have put them under more significant financial strain. They will also be more dependent 
on cost-of-living (COLA) increases in Social Security to underpin their spending.  
And, more broadly, outside of card spending, healthcare costs are a much larger factor in older households’ budgets (Exhibit 9). 
The evolution of these costs going forward will be a key variable for older consumers’ budgets.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts.  
Durables spending is defined as spending on electronics, building materials, auto-related spending and furniture. Premium durables spending is 
based on a selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively higher value products. Conversely, value durables spending is based on a 
selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively lower value products.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Our colleagues analyse today’s budget announcement in France in
this note. ING’s view is that this budget buys some time but fails to
solve structural deﬁcit issues. Markets still have little incentive to

back into an environment where the euro becomes much more

so far been remarkably muted compared with those earlier periods.

EUR downside risks increasing
That is mostly bad news for EUR/USD, with a couple of caveats. The
negative aspect is that there is still plenty of room to catch up with
the ﬁscal story. The chart above suggests a 3% risk premium
buildup on the euro would be entirely in line with previous history,
meaning a move to 1.110 is a possibility in a scenario of further
spread widening, even if not extreme.
The positive caveat is that the limited EUR reaction may suggest

that markets are not as concerned as they were about the Italian
ﬁscal trajectory. The US is admittedly facing its own deal of ﬁscal
scrutiny on top of that, and part of the French spread widening has
surely followed higher energy prices, which as we know can fall
quite rapidly in an improved geopolitical landscape.
EUR/CHF is historically the cleanest way to hedge the eurozone
ﬁscal risk, and we could see further downside there too. Still, the
franc’s idiosyncratic fragilities may get in the way, meaning we

don’t expect CHF to outperform USD materially purely on the back
of the French story. A drop to 1.110 in EUR/USD could equal a move
to roughly 0.920-0.925 in EUR/CHF.

## Document 234: German Defense_ An Ecosystem in Transformation (2026-09-25)

Source PDF: all report pdf/German Defense_ An Ecosystem in Transformation (2026-09-25).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-09-25
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

German Defense:  
An Ecosystem in Transformation

1 
Authors 
Michael Kuhn 
Research Analyst

Tim Rokossa 
Head of European Equity Research

Christoph Laskawi 
Research Analyst

Christophe Menard 
Research Analyst

Sriram Krishnan, CFA 
Research Analyst

September 25, 2026 
This is the first section of a report available to clients via our Deutsche Bank 
Research site here. 
Launching Private Company Analysis with the German Defense Sector 
Traditional analysis that focused on public companies overlooked a critical 
market segment: the many important competitors that are privately owned, 
typically by families or private equity firms. To address this, we are launching 
dedicated analysis of Europe’s private-market giants, starting with Germany, 
where a traditionally dominant private sector means large firms outnumber 
public ones almost two to one. We begin with the German defense sector, where 
understanding the private ecosystem is essential: it is the source of critical 
innovation, the solution to supply-chain issues, and the venue for a future wave 
of M&A, consolidation, and IPOs. 
From Incremental Modernization to Industrial Scaling 
Germany's defense landscape is undergoing its most significant transformation 
in decades, shifting from a paradigm of 'incremental modernization' to 'industrial 
scaling' . Triggered by the Zeitenwende in 2022, the new era is underpinned by 
substantial financial commitments, including a €100bn special fund and 
constitutional reforms enabling sustained long-term spending. The objective is 
no longer merely to fill discrete capability gaps but to execute a full 'ecosystem 
build-out' that addresses structural choke points and enhances industrial 
capacity. This transformation is creating a more resilient and scalable German 
Defense Industrial Base (DIB), prepared to meet surging domestic and European 
demand. 
Technology, Integration, and the Rise of New Players 
This strategic pivot is also reshaping the industrial and technological 
environment. EU initiatives (EDF , EDIRPA, ASAP) are fostering joint funding and 
integrated supply chains, while the market is experiencing a 'next wave' of 
technological change, with a clear shift in value towards drones, software-
defined systems, electronic warfare, and AI-driven capabilities. These dynamics 
force industry consolidation but also create openings for agile start-ups, which 
are vital for innovation, as speed and software become paramount. This report 
provides an in-depth primer on this evolving sector. We identify Germany’s 
industrial strengths and technology gaps, analyze the key private players poised 
to benefit, and examine the structural themes shaping the market - from funding 
dynamics and procurement reforms to technology roadmaps.

### Page 2

25 September 2026
Aerospace & Defence
Defense
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
Half the Industry is Invisible
We are Changing that.
Equity research has always looked at the listed half of the economy. That half is
shrinking. Companies stay private for longer, the largest capital pools are now
private, and in Germany the listed universe was never the industry to begin with.
There are roughly 120 listed German companies with sales above €1bn - and
roughly 200 unlisted ones.  In our home market, large private companies

outnumber public ones almost two to one.
That gap is not a curiosity. It is an analytical problem.
If a sector's innovation, its supply-chain frictions, its pricing behavior and its
eventual consolidation sit largely outside the listed universe, then covering only the
listed names means covering only part of the argument. With this report we start
private-market giants, beginning in Germany.
Why we are starting with German defense
Defense is the sharpest version of the problem we are trying to solve. Germany is

in the middle of its largest rearmament cycle since reunification, but much of the
capability being bought is not primarily produced by the listed defense groups. It is
produced by an ecosystem: family-owned system houses that have supplied the
Bundeswehr for decades, venture-capital-backed companies that did not exist
three years ago, and the tier-2 and tier-3 suppliers who sit on the actual constraints
- energetics, seekers, qualified electronics, test capacity, cleared labor. That

ecosystem is where the innovation happens, where the supply chain either breaks
Understanding it is not a side-project to covering Rheinmetall, Hensoldt or Renk. It
is a prerequisite.
Executive Summary - Eight Points Carry this Report
1.  The spending shift is structural, not cyclical. The €100bn special fund was
the headline; the 2025 constitutional reform exempting defense spending
above 1% of GDP from the debt brake was the substance. Government

budgets now foresee more than €150bn of annual defense spending by
2029, against roughly €52bn of core budget in 2024. NATO's 2025 pledge
of 3.5% of GDP on core defense by 2035 puts a floor under the back-end.
2.  Budgets are not revenue. Between an approved budget and a delivered
capability sit parliamentary approval for anything above €25m, tender
design, contracting, qualification, test capacity and acceptance. The
February 2026 procurement acceleration reform helps, but the conversion

chain - not demand - sets the delivery slope.
3.  Value is moving to software, autonomy and effects. Drones, software-
defined systems, electronic warfare and AI-enabled decision layers are
taking share from platform-centric spend. That favors companies whose
iteration cycle is measured in weeks, and it is the clearest reason private
and venture-capital-backed firms now sit inside mainstream force design.
4.  Production is deterrence - and production is where Germany is short.

Scaling breaks at specific points: energetics permits, seekers and
guidance electronics, qualification burden, specialized labor, and limited
or scales.
closing that gap. We are launching dedicated, ongoing analysis of Europe's

### Page 3

25 September 2026
Aerospace & Defence
Defense
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
test windows. Almost every one of those nodes is occupied, at least in part,
by a private company.
5.  The private ecosystem is now procurement-relevant, not just innovation-
relevant. The February 2026 Bundeswehr loitering-munitions framework,
awarded to Helsing and STARK from an initial package of roughly €540m,
is the clearest proof point. Sovereign buyers are contracting with

companies two to five years old.
6.  Scale still sits with the incumbents. Diehl generated €5.4bn of sales and
€703m of EBIT in FY2025, with Defence at roughly €2.3bn; Rohde &
Schwarz passed €3bn of revenue in FY2024/25 with no bank debt. These
are not start-ups with a defense angle - they are among Europe's more
significant defense industrial assets, and they happen to be family-owned.
Regulation is the binding constraint, in both directions. Export licensing,

foreign investment screening, NATO interoperability and compliance
intensity limit scaling and exits. But policy also accelerates - fast-track
procurement, EU joint programs, state-backed vehicles for defense tech.
Why defense, in numbers
The money. Germany's core defense budget has moved from roughly €47bn in
2021 to a projected path above €150bn by 2029, with the €100bn special fund
bridging the middle of that ramp. The 2025 debt-brake reform matters more than

the fund itself: by exempting defense spending above 1% of GDP, it converts a one-
off program into a sustained baseline. Procurement approvals have already
responded: 97 major defense projects approved in 2024 against 55 in 2023, with
over €58bn allocated.

The conversion chain. What is in doubt is throughput. Contracts above €25m
require binding Bundestag budget-committee approval, and tendering, supplier
orders, integration, testing and acceptance each add time. Europe's fragmentation
compounds it: European states operate around 179 different major weapon
systems against 33 in the US, and 19 different main battle tanks against one.

Between 2020 and 2024, European arms imports rose 155%, and European NATO
members sourced 64% of those imports from the US, up from 52% in 2015-19.
Whether 'buy European' becomes policy rather than rhetoric is the single largest
swing factor for the domestic industrial base.
Figure 2: Budget to Output: The Conversion Chain
Source : Deutsche Bank research
Where the private sector sits. The constrained nodes are consistent across almost
every program: energetics capacity, defense-qualified electronics, test and

certification, and cleared labor. Private companies are disproportionately present in
exactly those nodes. Diehl's acquisition of Dynamit Nobel was, in substance, the
acquisition of explosives-related operating authorizations it describes as unique in
Germany. STARK's dependence on TDW for German-manufactured warheads is
the same story one tier down. Rohde & Schwarz occupies the secure-
communications and SIGINT/EW layer no platform program can route around.

Figure 3: German Defense Industrial Value Chain
Source : Deutsche Bank research

25 September 2026
Aerospace & Defence
Defense
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Where the new money is going. Germany attracted close to €1bn of defense and
security venture-capital funding in 2025, second only to the US. The deal mix since
2021: unmanned systems (34 deals), surveillance and space (24), other defense
tech (24), electronic warfare (10) and cyber (8). Helsing raised $1.8bn at an $18bn
valuation in July 2026; STARK Defence raised €500m in June 2026 above €3.5bn.

Capital of that scale changes the industrial map.

The five debates - and where we go next
1.  Can Germany convert budget into capability fast enough?  Demand is
settled; throughput is not. Watch acceptance capacity, qualification

timelines and framework design rather than headline budgets.
2.  Do the newcomers industrialize, or stall at the factory door?  Helsing,
STARK Defence, ARX Robotics and Quantum Systems are all making the
same transition from software and prototypes into serial hardware. That is
where defense start-ups historically fail. The next 18 months are the test.
3.  Do incumbents get disrupted, or absorb? The likelier outcome is a denser
partner network - Diehl with Hensoldt and Airbus, Helsing with Saab and

Kongsberg, ARX Robotics with RENK and Daimler Truck. Partnership maps
are becoming a better guide to competitive position than product catalogs.
4.  Does 'buy European' become real? A conscious substitution of even part
of the 64% US import share would be a structural repricing of the European
industrial base. Fragmentation and interoperability standards cut against
it.
5.  Who is financed, and how? Rising capital intensity, thin private disclosure,

constrained exit routes under foreign-investment screening, and uneven
ESG treatment will all influence the financing and consolidation wave.

### Page 6

25 September 2026
Aerospace & Defence
Defense
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
The Argument in Five Parts
1. The Strategic Landscape: Russia's 2022 invasion of Ukraine, combined with the
distancing of the US as Europe's protector, represents a turning point for the safety
of Europe and its defense industry. The shift in policy is creating immense demand
across Europe for arms in the decades to come, leading to significant M&A and
investing opportunities. German companies are uniquely suited to serve the rising

demand due to their immense technical know-how and their already-existing
industrial sector. Combined with targeted funding programs, these developments
both stimulate existing companies and incentivize start-ups that push the technical
frontier forward.
2. New Wars Change the Old Battlefield: Recent wars in Ukraine and the Middle
East have reshaped modern warfare, shifting capital from traditional contractors to
start-ups that can keep pace with rapid innovation cycles. Key trends defining this

new era include: unmanned systems creating a transparent battlefield that
demands adaptability; huge munitions consumption proving that production itself
is deterrence; the battlespace expanding into commercial space and cyberspace;
and societal resilience against disinformation becoming a vital security element.
This all occurs amid a new great power competition, heightening the risk of multi-
theater wars and the use of nuclear threats to back conventional aggression.

3. How to Gain Exposure – Investment Opportunities in Private Gems:  Rapid
innovation in modern warfare is creating significant investment opportunities in
Germany's defense sector, as start-ups outperform slow, traditional procurement.
This operational shift is fueled by a long-term surge in European defense spending
targeted at R&D and equipment. Investors can gain exposure through key 'private
gems', from established firms like Diehl Group and Rohde & Schwarz to high-

growth start-ups like Helsing (AI) and Quantum Systems (drones). These start-ups
leverage a mix of venture capital and government grants with innovative business
models, making the German private defense market a compelling investment area.
4. Trend – Europe Buys European & Europe Buys between Middle Powers: Recent
energy and security shocks have shattered Europe's post-Cold War assumptions,
creating an urgent push for strategic self-reliance amid uncertainty over the long-

term US security guarantee. This pivot is evident in new security partnerships, such
as Germany deepening defense ties in the Indo-Pacific (Japan) and North Atlantic
(Canada). Paradoxically, Europe's rearmament has increased its reliance on US
hardware (64% of imports '20-'24) and its defense sector remains highly
fragmented. The core challenge is whether Europe's quest for autonomy will lead
to a 'buy European' policy; doing so would unlock a monumental internal market,

driving consolidation and creating a truly independent defense capability.
5. The Regulatory Landscape & Investment Constraints: The defense industry is
uniquely constrained and regulated, and investors must understand its landscape.
Growth is not driven simply by demand but is filtered through government budgets,
procurement laws, and export controls. This creates risks: budgets don't translate
to immediate revenue, market access can be denied politically, and production

cannot be scaled freely. High compliance costs and national security reviews that
limit exit strategies add further complexity. Successful investment, therefore,
requires mastering these political and regulatory realities, not just identifying
demand.

### Page 5

5

Tăng trưởng tín dụng 2026 được dự 
báo khả quan so với cùng kỳ  
 Chúng tôi duy trì dự báo tăng trưởng tín dụng cả năm 2026 ở mức 27%, vượt 
trội hơn mặt bằng chung của ngành nhờ lợi thế tham gia nhận CGBB. Cụ thể: 
− Xét về phân khúc khách hàng, động lực chính kỳ vọng sẽ đến từ phân 
khúc KHDN, trong đó nổi bật là nhóm FDI khi ngân hàng đã chủ động 
thành lập khối Khách hàng FDI chuyên biệt trong bối cảnh dòng vốn 
FDI vào Việt Nam và hoạt động xuất nhập khẩu duy trì tích cực.  
− Xét về lĩnh vực, chúng tôi cho rằng cho vay kinh doanh BĐS sẽ tăng 
trưởng vượt trội với kỳ vọng ngân hàng tham gia vào thu xếp các dự án 
hạ tầng và dự án BĐS ưu tiên (BĐS KCN, NOXH). Ngân hàng cho biết tỷ 
trọng dư nợ mảng này dự kiến sẽ được duy trì trong biên độ mục tiêu 
13±2% tổng dư nợ tín dụng. Chúng tôi nghiêng về khả năng ngân hàng 
sẽ bám sát cận trên của mục tiêu (15%), nhằm tận dụng dư địa tăng 
trưởng từ các dự án ưu tiên, đồng thời duy trì kiểm soát chất lượng tài 
sản an toàn. Trong khi đó, dư nợ cho vay các mảng khác dự kiến sẽ ở 
mức thấp hơn tăng trưởng chung do môi trường vĩ mô còn nhiều khó 
khăn.

Biểu đồ 8. Cơ cấu tín dụng 
Tỷ trọng cho vay kinh doanh BĐS liên tục gia tăng 
trong các quý gần đây, trong khi cơ cấu cho vay 
thương mại và chế biến chế tạo có xu hướng giảm. 
Tính tới hết 1H2026, tăng trưởng cho vay kinh doanh 
BĐS, thương mại, và chế biến chế tạo lần lượt đạt 
38.2%, 4.9%, và 9.3% YTD.

Nguồn: MBB, KBSV

NIM chịu áp lực giảm so với 2025  Chúng tôi duy trì dự phóng NIM cả năm sẽ giảm so với 2025 (-15bps YoY) dựa 
trên luận điểm:  
(1) Ngân hàng sẽ tiếp tục dịch chuyển cơ cấu huy động sang các kênh có 
chi phí vốn (COF) cao hơn nhằm đáp ứng mục tiêu tăng trưởng tín 
dụng cao, trong bối cảnh cạnh tranh huy động trên thị trường 1 vẫn 
diễn ra gay gắt. Bên cạnh đó, ngân hàng cho biết dự kiến sẽ phát hành 
10-15 nghìn tỷ trái phiếu tier 2 trong 2H2026 với COF tương đối cao. 
(2) Với mục tiêu tăng trưởng tín dụng cao, chúng tôi cho rằng ngân hàng 
sẽ duy trì lãi suất cho vay ở mức cạnh tranh so với mặt bằng chung của 
ngành. Các khoản vay dự kiến vẫn sẽ tiếp tục tái định giá theo mặt 
bằng lãi suất hiện tại, tuy nhiên tốc độ sẽ chậm lại so với quý 2, do đó 
lợi suất IEA dự kiến sẽ tăng chậm hơn COF trong 2H2026.

Chất lượng tài sản suy giảm trong 
tầm kiểm soát  
 Trong 2Q2026, tỷ lệ hình thành nợ xấu ở mức 1.2% (-16bps QoQ), tương ứng 
4,142 tỷ đồng nợ xấu phát sinh mới, và tỷ lệ nợ xấu tăng nhẹ 3bps so với quý 
trước. Nợ xấu tăng trong quý này chủ yếu tới từ phân khúc bán lẻ (do hoạt động 
kinh doanh và giá đất của khách hàng tại địa phương bị ảnh hưởng sau khi sáp

Cho cả năm 2026, chúng tôi dự phóng tỷ lệ nợ xấu của ngân hàng sẽ tăng 11bps 
YoY trong bối cảnh lãi suất đang neo ở mức cao. Cùng với đó, chúng tôi kỳ vọng 
ngân hàng sẽ tiếp tục duy trì chính sách trích lập dự phòng thận trọng nhằm 
củng cố bộ đệm, qua đó hoàn thành mục tiêu duy trì tỷ lệ bao phủ nợ xấu ở mức 
trên 100%.

Định giá – Khuyến nghị: MUA – Giá 
mục tiêu 25,950 VNĐ/CP  
 Chúng tôi kết hợp 2 phương pháp định giá là P/B và Chiết khấu lợi nhuận thặng 
dư để tìm ra mức giá hợp lý cho cổ phiếu MBB. 
(1) Phương pháp định giá P/B 
Chúng tôi điều chỉnh P/B mục tiêu xuống mức 1.5x trong bối cảnh lãi 
suất neo cao và thanh khoản ngân hàng còn nhiều khó khăn. Dù vậy, 
P/B mục tiêu vẫn cao hơn so với P/B trung bình 5 năm của ngân 
hàng nhằm phản ánh kỳ vọng tích cực đối với việc room ngoại của 
ngân hàng được nới lên 49% theo Nghị định 69/2025/NĐ-CP. 
(2) Phương pháp chiết khấu lợi nhuận thặng dư  
Bên cạnh đó, chúng tôi kết hợp sử dụng thêm phương pháp chiết 
khấu lợi nhuận thặng dư để phản ánh rủi ro hệ thống và kỳ vọng dài 
hạn. 
Khuyến nghị MUA  với cổ phiếu MBB với giá mục tiêu 25,950 VNĐ/CP - tiềm 
năng tăng giá 26.2% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 17/09/2026.
